Stability of phase portrait for a gradient ODE with memory
Piotr Kalita, Piotr Zgliczyński

Abstract
. We consider the problem governed by the gradient ODE x ′ = ∇ F ​ ( x ) x^{\prime}=\nabla F(x) in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} on which we assume that it has a finite number of hyperbolic equilibria whose stable and unstable manifolds intersect transversally. This problem is perturbed by the memory term x ′ ​ ( t ) = ∇ F ​ ( x ⁡ ( t ) ) + ε ​ ∫ − ∞ t M ⁡ ( t − s ) ​ x ​ ( s ) ​ 𝑑 s x^{\prime}(t)=\nabla F(x(t))+\varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-s)x(s)\,ds where ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 is a small constant. The key result is that the structure of connections between the equilibria of the unperturbed problem is exactly preserved for a small ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 .



1.
Introduction.

This paper deals with the gradient ordinary differential equation in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

(1)

x^{\prime}(t)=\nabla F(x(t))\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ F\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

and its perturbation by the linear memory term though which the derivative of the unknown solution depends not only on the instantaneous value of the solution but also on its past values

(2)

x^{\prime}(t)=\nabla F(x(t))+\varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-s)x(s)\,ds.

Because of the presence of the distributed delay term, if we study the flow governed by (
2
), we need to consider it in an infinite dimensional space, containing functions defined in the time interval from minus infinity to the current time instance.

We make the following assumptions on the functions
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
M:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
:

(1)

There exists
R>0
and
C_{F}>0
such that if
|x|\geq R
then
-\nabla F(x)\cdot x\geq C_{F}|x|^{2}
.

(2)

The unperturbed equation (
1
) has finite number of equilibria, all of them being hyperbolic, and their stable and unstable manifolds intersect transversally.

(3)

The matrix
\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds
is symmetric and
\|M(s)\|\leq E\lambda_{min}(A(s))
for a constant
E>0
(cf. Assumption
2.5
) where
A\in C^{1}([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
with
A(s)
being symmetric and positive definite matrix for every
s\geq 0
such that

(A)

For almost every
s>0
and for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\left(\frac{dA(s)}{ds}x,x\right)\leq-C_{A}(A(s)x,x),

with a constant
C_{A}>0
cf. Assumption
2.1
.

(B)

For every
s\geq 0
we have

\frac{\lambda_{max}(A(s))}{\lambda_{min}(A(s))}\leq D,

with a constant
D>0
, cf. Assumption
2.3
.

The function
A
, which by (3)(A) must decay exponentially to zero as
t\to\infty
, defines the phase space for the memory term, it is the space

L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}=\left\{\eta:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\ \int_{0}^{\infty}(A(s)\eta(s),\eta(s))\,ds<\infty\right\}.

Now, the equation (
1
) defines the gradient dynamical system
S^{0}(t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
t\geq 0
. This dynamical system has a global attractor which
consists of the finite number of equilibria and their connections. It structure is represented as a graph of partial order, the vertexes of this graph correspond to the equilibria of the system. An edge from
e_{i}
to
e_{j}
exists in this graph it there exists a bounded solution of (
1
) which converges to
e_{i}
as time tends to minus infinity and to
e_{j}
as time tends to plus infinity.

The problem governed by (
2
) defines a dynamical system for
\varepsilon>0
denoted as
S^{\varepsilon}(t):L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where the space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
contains the current state of the system, and
L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}
its past. This system for
\varepsilon>0
is infinite dimensional.
The main result of the paper is the following theorem

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume (1)–(3) above. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
the dynamical system governed by (
2
) has a global attractor consisting of a finite number of equilibria and their connections. The graph that represents this system coincides with the graph for the unperturbed finite dimensional system
\{S^{0}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
.

The question is motivated by the results of
[
1
]
. There, the authors consider the infinite dimensional autonomous gradient dynamical system and they prove that upon small non-autonomous perturbation the structure of its attractor is preserved, that is, the phase portrait of the non-autonomous dynamics coincides with the autonomous one. Thus, the authors in
[
1
]
are able to fully characterize the non-autonomous dynamics for the problem which is small perturbation of the autonomous one.
Our result is of similar nature as
[
1
]
, but our main contribution stands in the fact that the unperturbed system is finite-dimensional and the perturbed one - infinite dimensional. This infinite dimensionality manifests itself in the presence of the memory term in the perturbed system.

The proof that the structure of connections is exactly preserved upon perturbation consists of three ingredients:

(A)

the equilibria of the perturbed problem exist in the vicinity of the equilibria of the original one, and that these are all equilibria, cf. Theorem
5.1
,

(B)

no new connections arise when
\varepsilon>0
, i.e. the connections structure behaves upper-semicontinuously, cf. Theorem
5.5
,

(C)

the existing connections are preserved upon perturbation, i.e. the connections structure behaves lower-semicontinuously, cf. Theorem
6.9
.

Fundamental ingredient in the proofs of these items is the fact, obtained in Sections
3
and
4
, that certain dynamical properties of the unperturbed problem can be continued for
\varepsilon>0
. In particular it is possible to construct the common Lyapunov function for
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. Moreover, we construct isolating blocks: i.e. the sets which isolate the equilibria of (
1
), which after taking the Cartesian product with a certain ball in the memory space
L^{2}_{A}(R^{+})^{d}
also isolate the equilibria of (
2
) with entry and exit behavior on the boundary being uniform with respect to
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. We prove that the new, infinite dimensional, variable
\eta\in L^{2}_{A}(R^{+})^{d}
can be bundled together with the stable variables belonging to the finite dimensional state
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Finally, we prove that the cone condition holds in these isolating sets with the same system of coordinates and the same quadratic form in the range of small
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
.
This opens the possibility of using the Hadamard’s graph transform procedure to construct the local stable and unstable manifolds of the equilibria as the Lipschitz graphs over the same systems of coordinates in the considered range of
\varepsilon
. The above assertion (A) follows from the construction of common isolating blocks with the cone condition, and (B) follows from the compactness argument (similar as in
[
1
]
), these results are contained in Section
5
.
To get the most involved result (C), we need to prove that the local stable and unstable manifolds are actually
C^{1}
close to each other in dependence on
\varepsilon
. We prove this by the differentiation of the graph transform. Moreover, we transport the smallness of
C^{1}
distance between the local unstable manifolds along the flow in order to prove that the transversality of the intersection for
\varepsilon=0
implies that this intersection is preserved upon the perturbation. This argument needs careful handling of the memory variable which appears in the system for
\varepsilon>0
. The result is contained in Section
6
.

The fact that the norm of the memory term is weighted by the expression that decays exponentially to zero is a fundamental fact which allows us to treat the memory variable as the stable variable in the neighborhood of the equilibrium. The key result here is the dissipative estimate (
13
) in Lemma
3.2
on the time evolution of the norm of memory variable which is obtained in Section
3.2
. This estimate is derived using the concept from the seminal paper of Dafermos
[
5
]
, who proved that in the linear problem of viscoelasticity the memory term is dissipative and has damping effect on the solution, which decays to zero due to this term’s presence. Discoveries of Dafermos were later used in the context of global attractors for the nonlinear problem of viscoelasticity by Conti and Pata
[
3
]
, who explored the dissipative nature of the memory term to obtain the existence of the global attractor. Dissipativity of the memory term in the context of global attractors has also been explored for the first order, reaction-diffusion type, problems in
[
4
,
10
,
11
]
, where the authors showed that it is possible to proceed directly using the variables of the system, without the Dafermos transformation. All these results, however, are of global nature. The novel contribution of this paper, is the exploitation of the dissipative nature of the memory term in the local argument realized in the neighbourhood of the equilibria and its application to recover the full intrinsic structure of the global attractor.

Another novelty of this paper is the development of the geometric methods to study the dynamics of the problem with memory/delay. Locally, in the neighbourhood of the equilibrium we apply the Hadamard procedure to obtain the existence of local stable and unstable manifolds. Then, we show that local unstable manifolds for the perturbed problems are
C^{1}
-close to those of the original problem. We continue this proximity along the solutions, in order to prove that these manifolds are
C^{1}
close near the point of intersection, which lies in the neighborhood of the target point of the heteroclinic connection. Thus, we develop a geometric approach for problems with memory, which is useful for studying the dynamics of the problem. We stress that while we apply these methods to a particular class of problems, we expect that the proposed techniques can be extended to a wider class of problems with memory and/or delay. In particular, while we prove that the structure of connections in a gradient system is preserved under perturbation, we expect that our techniques will allow us to prove more general results, demonstrating that the dynamics of the problem remains unchanged under perturbation by the memory term. This is possible because, in our framework, the additional memory variable is, in some sense, damped. Moreover, as an interesting open question we ask, we ask, whether it is possible to study, in a similar way, the dynamics for the case where the kernel in the memory term does not decay exponentially, but, for example, polynomially.

One of the ingredients of our proof is the result on the existence of local stable and unstable manifolds. While we use the graph transform method, the alternative approach - the Lyapunov–Perron method - has been used to obtain the similar types of results for the problems with finite delay in
[
8
]
. We note that the results on the attractor structure for problems with delay or memory are not frequent: we refer for example to
[
12
]
for an overview of results and list of relevant references or to
[
9
]
for the study of fine structure of the unstable manifold of a periodic solution for finitely delayed ODE.

Due to the fact that some of the arguments of the article are technical, they are moved to Appendices. Appendix 1 contains the results on asymptotic compactness of the dynamical system with memory. Appendix 2 is devoted to the Hadamard’s graph transform procedure and the results on the existence of local stable and unstable manifolds. In Appendix 3 the results on
C^{0}
dependence of those manifolds on parameter
\varepsilon
are proved. Appendix 4 presents the approach to graph transform method using the Banach fixed point theorem, this argument allows us to differentiate the graph transform map and, using the fiber contraction argument, obtain the
C^{1}
smoothness of stable and unstable manifolds. In Appendix 5 the abstract cone conditions from Appendix 4 are demonstrated to hold for the considered problem. Finally, Appendix 6 is devoted to the proof that the derivatives of local stable and unstable manifolds depend continuously on the parameter
\varepsilon
.

2.
The weighted history space and its norm.

Let
A:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a time dependent matrix function. This function will be used in the definition of the norm in the history space. Assumptions
2.1
,
2.3
on the function
A
and Assumption
2.5
on related matrix function
M
will be standing assumptions throughout the whole article.

Assumption 2.1
.

Assume that
A(s)
is a symmetric and positive definite matrix for
s\geq 0
,
[0,\infty)\ni s\mapsto A(s)
belongs to
C^{1}([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
and that for almost every
s>0
and every
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3)

\left(\frac{dA(s)}{ds}u,u\right)\leq-C_{A}(A(s)u,u).

Lemma 2.2
.

Under Assumption
2.1
we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds<\infty

Proof.

We have

e^{C_{A}s}\frac{d}{ds}(A(s)u,u)+C_{A}e^{C_{A}s}(A(s)u,u)\leq 0,

for every
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Hence

\frac{d}{ds}(e^{C_{A}s}(A(s)u,u))\leq 0,

and

e^{C_{A}s}(A(s)u,u)\leq(A(0)u,u),\quad s\geq 0

Finally

(A(s)u,u)\leq e^{-C_{A}s}(A(0)u,u),

for every
s\geq 0
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
As
A(s)
is symmetric and positively definite then for every
s
we can find a vector
u(s)
with norm one such that

\|A(s)\|=(A(s)u(s),u(s))\leq e^{-C_{A}s}(A(0)u(s),u(s))\leq\|A(0)\|e^{-C_{A}s},

and the assertion follows.
∎

We define the space
L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}
with the norm
\|\eta\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}(A(s)\eta(s),\eta(s))\,ds.

Assumption 2.3
.

Assume that for some constant
\overline{D_{A}}>0
and every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

(4)

\|A(s)\|\ |x|^{2}\leq\overline{D_{A}}^{2}(A(s)x,x).

In other words

\frac{\lambda_{max}(A(s))}{\lambda_{min}(A(s))}\leq\overline{D_{A}}^{2}\ \ \textrm{for every}\ \ s\geq 0.

Lemma 2.4
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.3
for every
\eta\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta(s)\,ds\right|\leq\left(\overline{D_{A}}\sqrt{\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds}\right)\|\eta\|:=D_{A}\|\eta\|.

Proof.

We begin with the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta(s)\,ds\right|^{2}\leq\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,|\eta(s)|\,ds\right)^{2}.

By the Hölder inequality

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta(s)\,ds\right|^{2}\leq\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\|A(s)\|}\sqrt{\|A(s)\|}\,|\eta(s)|\,ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,|\eta(s)|^{2}\,ds\leq\overline{D_{A}}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds\int_{0}^{\infty}(A(s)\eta(s),\eta(s))\,ds.

and the proof is complete.
∎

Now consider the function
M:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. We make the following assumption

Assumption 2.5
.

Assume that for every
s\geq 0

(5)

{\|M(s)\|}\leq\overline{D_{M}}^{2}\lambda_{min}(A(s)),

with a constant
\overline{D_{M}}>0
and

\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\ \ \mathrm{is\ symmetric}.

Remark 2.6
.

The fact that
\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds
is finite follows from (
5
) and the assumptions on
A
. The symmetry of this integral is needed only to construct the Lyapunov function in Lemma
3.4
, and although it appears technical and having no natural explanation, it is unclear to us how to avoid this assumption in the argument.

The next result holds analogously to Lemma
2.4

Lemma 2.7
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.5
we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds<\infty

and

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta(s)\,ds\right|\leq D_{M}\|\eta\|\ \ \ \textrm{for every}\ \ \ \eta\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d},

for every
\eta\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}
, where
D_{M}=\overline{D_{M}}\sqrt{\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds}
.

An example of
A(s)
which satisfies the above assumptions is
A(s)=e^{-\kappa s}I
. Then
C_{A}=\kappa
,
\overline{D_{A}}=1
, and we need, in addition to the symmetry of the integral of
M
that

\|M(s)\|\leq\overline{D_{M}}^{2}e^{-\kappa s}.

3.
Problem setup and its basic properties

3.1.
Unperturbed and perturbed problems. Dafermos transform and memory variable.

We consider the following ODE

(6)

x^{\prime}(t)=f(x(t))\quad\textrm{where}\quad f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We assume that the ODE has a gradient form, i.e.

(7)

f(x)=\nabla F(x)\ \ \textrm{where}\ \ F\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Moreover we assume that

(8)

\text{there exist constants}\ R>0,C_{F}>0\ \text{such that if}\ |x|\geq R\ \text{then}\ -\nabla F(x)\cdot x\geq C_{F}|x|^{2}.

We need a following simple property

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume (
8
). Then there exist constants
\gamma>0
and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(9)

F(x)\leq-\gamma|x|^{2}+\delta.

Proof.

If
|x|\leq R
then for every constant
\gamma>0
we have

F(x)\leq\max_{|x|\leq R}F(x)\leq-\gamma|x|^{2}+{\gamma}R^{2}+\max_{|x|\leq R}F(x)=-\gamma|x|^{2}+\delta_{1}(\gamma).

If
|x|>R
then
x=(1+c)x_{0}
for some
|x_{0}|=\frac{R}{2}
with
c>1
. Then

\displaystyle F(x)=F(x_{0})+\int_{0}^{1}\nabla F(x_{0}(1+\theta c))cx_{0}\,d\theta=F(x_{0})+\int_{0}^{1}\nabla F(x_{0}(1+\theta c))\frac{1+\theta c}{1+\theta c}cx_{0}\,d\theta

\displaystyle\ \ \leq F(x_{0})-C_{F}\int_{0}^{1}|x_{0}|^{2}c(1+\theta c)\,d\theta=F(x_{0})-C_{F}\int_{0}^{1}|x_{0}|^{2}c(1+\theta c)\,d\theta

\displaystyle\ \ =F(x_{0})-C_{F}|x_{0}|^{2}c\left(1+\frac{c}{2}\right)=F(x_{0})-C_{F}|x|^{2}\frac{c\left(2+c\right)}{2(1+c)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{|x|\leq R}F(x)-\frac{3}{8}C_{F}|x|^{2},

and the proof is complete.
∎

We perturb the above ODE with the additive linear distributed delay term that is multiplied by a small parameter
\varepsilon>0
. This yields the equation

(10)

x^{\prime}(t)=f(x(t))+\varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-s)x(s)\,ds,

where
M(s)=\{M_{ij}(s)\}_{i,j=1}^{d}
is a time dependent matrix.

Rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(t)=f(x(t))+\varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-s)(x(s)-x(t)+x(t))\,ds

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ =f(x(t))+\varepsilon\left(\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-s)\,ds\right)x(t)+\varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-s)(x(s)-x(t))\,ds.

After change of variables in time integrals we obtain

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(t)=f(x(t))+\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)x(t)+\varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{t}M(t-s)(x(s)-x(t))\,ds.

The Dafermos transform consists in using the correspondence between functions
x:(-\infty,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and pairs
(\eta,x_{0})
with
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\eta:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
x_{0}=x(t)
and
\eta(s)=x(t-s)-x(t)
. The inverse of this correspondence maps the pair
(\eta,x_{0})
to the function
x(s)=x_{0}+\eta(t-s)
for
s\leq t
. Thus, we introduce the new variable

\eta^{t}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
\eta^{t}(s)=x(t-s)-x(t)
.
Taking into account the influence of the initial data, we define

(11)

\eta^{t}(s)=\begin{cases}x(t-s)-x(t)\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ s\leq t\\
x(t-s)-x(t)=x_{0}+\eta^{0}(s-t)-x(t)\ \ \textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Using this variable, the equation of the problem takes the form

(12)

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(t)=f^{\varepsilon}(x(t))+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(x(t-s)-x(t))\,ds=f^{\varepsilon}(x(t))+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,

where
f^{\varepsilon}(x)=f(x)+\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)x
, and equation (
11
) governs the evolution of
\eta^{t}
.

3.2.
Energy inequality for the variable
\eta
.

The following lemma plays a crucial role in passing from ODE (
6
) to (
10
) as it shows that the tail
\eta
can be treated
as a "contracting" direction from the point of view of geometric methods in dynamics. This is a crucial fact from the point of view of the tools used in this paper.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let Assumption
2.1
hold and let
x\in C^{1}([0,\infty))^{d}
. Moreover let
\eta^{t}
for
t\in[0,\infty)
be given by (
11
) with the initial data
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\eta^{0}\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}
. Then
\eta\in C^{1}([0,\infty);L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d})
,

(13)

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}\leq-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right),

and

(14)

\|\eta^{t_{2}}\|^{2}\leq e^{-C_{A}(t_{2}-t_{1})}\|\eta^{t_{1}}\|^{2}-2e^{-C_{A}t_{2}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}e^{C_{A}t}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right)\,dt\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ t_{1}<t_{2}.

Proof.

Let
\eta^{0}\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}
,
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
x\in C^{1}([0,\infty))^{d}
. Define
x(-s)=x_{0}+\eta^{0}(s)
for
s>0
. If
\eta^{t}
is given by (
11
) then the squared norm of
\eta^{t}
is given by

\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}(A(s)(x(t-s)-x(t)),(x(t-s)-x(t)))\,ds=\int_{-\infty}^{t}(A(t-s)(x(s)-x(t)),(x(s)-x(t)))\,ds.

Let
t\geq 0
and
h>0
. We calculate the right derivative of the above squared norm with respect to
t
.

\displaystyle\frac{\|\eta^{t+h}\|^{2}-\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}}{h}=\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}(A(t+h-s)(x(s)-x(t+h)),(x(s)-x(t+h)))\,ds

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +\int_{-\infty}^{t}\left(\frac{A(t+h-s)-A(t-s)}{h}(x(s)-x(t)),(x(s)-x(t))\right)\,ds

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +h\left(\int_{-\infty}^{t}A(t+h-s)\,ds\frac{x(t)-x(t+h)}{h},\frac{x(t)-x(t+h)}{h}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +2\left(\int_{-\infty}^{t}A(t+h-s)(x(s)-x(t))\,ds,\frac{x(t)-x(t+h)}{h}\right).

Passing to the limit with
h\to 0^{+}
, using the mean value theorem for integrals, the first term in the above sum tends to zero. Moreover, the limit of the third term is zero. In the second and fourth term we use the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem to pass to the limit, whence

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\|\eta^{t+h}\|^{2}-\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}}{h}

\displaystyle\ \ =\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{A(s+h)-A(s)}{h}(x(t-s)-x(t)),(x(t-s)-x(t))\right)\,ds

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ -2\left(\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s+h)(x(t-s)-x(t))\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ =\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{dA(s)}{ds}\eta^{t}(s),\eta^{t}(s)\right)\,ds-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right).

Similar calculation for
t>0
and
h<0
leads to the left derivative for
t>0
.
Hence

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{dA(s)}{ds}\eta^{t}(s),\eta^{t}(s)\right)\,ds-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right),

and the assertion (
13
) follows by Assumption
2.1
.
After multiplication by the integrating factor
e^{C_{A}t}
we deduce

\frac{d}{dt}e^{C_{A}t}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}\,dt\leq-2e^{C_{A}t}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right)

Integrating from
t_{1}
to
t_{2}
we obtain (
14
).
∎

Similar argument leads to the following result

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
x,y\in C^{1}([0,\infty)]
and let
\eta,\xi
be given by (
11
) for
x
and
y
, respectively. Then

(15)

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}\leq-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta^{t}(s)-\xi^{t}(s))\,ds,(x(t)-y(t))^{\prime}\right)

and

\displaystyle\|\eta^{t_{2}}-\xi^{t_{2}}\|^{2}\leq e^{-C_{A}(t_{2}-t_{1})}\|\eta^{t_{1}}-\xi^{t_{1}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-2e^{-C_{A}t_{2}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}e^{C_{A}t}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta^{t}(s)-\xi^{t}(s))\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)-y^{\prime}(t)\right)\,dt\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ t_{1}<t_{2}.

3.3.
Lyapunov function for the problem governed by (
10
) and (
11
).

The goal of this subsection is to construct a Lyapunov function which is uniform with respect to small
\varepsilon\geq 0
, i.e. which is valid not only for the ODE which is a gradient system (i.e. with
f=\nabla F
) but also for (
10
) with sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. To this end let
\mathcal{T}
be a time interval either equal to
[0,T)
or
[0,\infty)
and assume that
(\eta,x)\in C^{1}(\mathcal{T};L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d})\times C^{1}(\mathcal{T})^{d}
solve (
10
)– (
11
). We have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

There exists
E_{0}>0
such that for every
E\in(0,E_{0})
there exists
\varepsilon_{0}(E)>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0})
we have

(16)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(E\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}-2F(x(t))-\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\,x(t),x(t)\right)\right)+|x^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+E\frac{C_{A}}{4}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}\leq 0\ \ \text{for}\ t\in\mathcal{T}.

Proof.

Multiply (
12
) by
2x^{\prime}(t)
. Then

2|x^{\prime}(t)|^{2}=2(f^{\varepsilon}(x(t)),x^{\prime}(t))+2\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right).

Adding this equation to the inequality (
13
) from Lemma
3.2
multiplied by
E>0
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}+2|x^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+EC_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}\leq 2(f(x(t)),x^{\prime}(t))

(17)

\displaystyle\ \ +2\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\ x(t),x^{\prime}(t)\right)-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\right)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right).

Using the fact that
f=\nabla F
and
\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds
is symmetric we get

(18)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(E\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}-2F(x(t))-\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\,x(t),x(t)\right)\right)+2|x^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+EC_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\right)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right).

Choosing
\delta>0
we estimate the term on the right-hand side as

2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\right)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,x^{\prime}(t)\right)\leq\delta|x^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+\frac{1}{\delta}\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\right)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds\right|^{2}

.
The last term can be estimated as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\right)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds\right|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\|EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\|}\sqrt{\|EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\|}\,|\eta^{t}(s)|\,ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\ \leq\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\|\,ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\|EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\|\,|\eta^{t}(s)|^{2}\,ds.

Moreover,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\|\,ds\leq E\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds,

and, using Assumptions
2.3
and
2.5

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|EA(s)-\varepsilon M(s)\|\,|\eta^{t}(s)|^{2}\,ds\leq E\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,|\eta^{t}(s)|^{2}\,ds+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,|\eta^{t}(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\ \ \leq\left(E\overline{D_{A}}^{2}+\varepsilon\overline{D_{M}}^{2}\right)\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}

Choosing
\delta=1
we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(E\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}-2F(x(t))-\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\,x(t),x(t)\right)\right)+|x^{\prime}(t)|^{2}+EC_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq\left(E\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\right)\left(E\overline{D_{A}}^{2}+\varepsilon\overline{D_{M}}^{2}\right)\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}.

Moving all terms to the left, the constant in front of
\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}
is equal to

-E^{2}\overline{D_{A}}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds+E\left(C_{A}-\varepsilon\overline{D_{M}}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(s)\|\,ds-\varepsilon\overline{D_{A}}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\right)-\varepsilon^{2}\overline{D_{M}}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds.

We want this expression to be non-negative. It can be rewritten as

-E^{2}G_{1}+E(G_{2}-\varepsilon G_{3})-\varepsilon^{2}G_{4},

where
G_{1},G_{2},G_{3},G_{4}
are positive constants. Take
E_{0}=\frac{G_{2}}{2G_{1}}
. If
E\in(0,E_{0})
, then

-E^{2}G_{1}+E(G_{2}-\varepsilon G_{3})-\varepsilon^{2}G_{4}\geq EG_{2}-E\frac{G_{2}}{2}-\varepsilon\frac{G_{3}G_{2}}{2G_{1}}-\varepsilon^{2}G_{4}=E\frac{G_{2}}{2}-\varepsilon\frac{G_{3}G_{2}}{2G_{1}}-\varepsilon^{2}G_{4}.

Now take
\varepsilon_{0}(E)
such that

\varepsilon\frac{G_{3}G_{2}}{2G_{1}}+\varepsilon^{2}G_{4}\leq E\frac{G_{2}}{4},

if only
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
.
This means that

-E^{2}G_{1}+E(G_{2}-\varepsilon G_{3})-\varepsilon^{2}G_{4}\geq E\frac{G_{2}}{4}=E\frac{C_{A}}{4}.

The proof is complete.
∎

As a consequence of the above Lemma we obtained the following Lyapunov function

L_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
valid for every
E\in(0,E_{0})
and for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}(E))

(19)

L_{\varepsilon}(\eta,x)=E\|\eta\|^{2}-2F(x)-\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\,x,x\right)

Observe that (
9
) implies that

(20)

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}(\eta,x)\geq E\|\eta\|^{2}+\left(2\gamma-\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\,\right)|x|^{2}-2\delta.

3.4.
Solution boundedness and
C^{0}
dependence on initial data.

We skip the proof of the existence and uniqueness of local solution for every initial data
(\eta^{0},x_{0})\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times R^{d}
. The fact that every solution can be extended to a global one follows from the next lemma which is a consequence of the existence of a Lyapunov function obtained in Lemma
3.4
.
We prove that every solution
(x,\eta)
of (
10
) and (
11
) is globally bounded by a constant dependent on the initial data.

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume (
7
) and (
9
). Under assumptions of Lemma
3.4
, if only

(21)

\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds<2\gamma,

then every solution is bounded uniformly on bounded sets of initial data.

Proof.

From
the Lyapunov function (
19
) and the bound (
20
) we obtain that

C(|x_{0}|,\|\eta^{0}\|)\geq L_{\varepsilon}(\eta^{0},x_{0})\geq L_{\varepsilon}(\eta^{t},x(t))\geq E\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}+\left(2\gamma-\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\,\right)|x(t)|^{2}-2\delta,

where
C(\cdot,\cdot)
is a continuous function independent of
\varepsilon
. This yields the assertion of the lemma.
∎

In the next result, we prove the Lipschitz continuous dependence of the solution on the initial data.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume (
7
) and (
9
). There exists
\varepsilon_{0}
such that for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
if
(\eta^{t},x(t))
and
(\xi^{t},x(t))
are two solutions with the initial data
(\eta^{0},x_{0})
and
(\xi^{0},y_{0})
, respectively, then for every
T>0
there exists a constant
L(T)
such that for every
t\in[0,T]
we have

|x(t)-y(t)|+\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|\leq L(T)(|x_{0}-y_{0}|+\|\eta^{0}-\xi^{0}\|).

Proof.

In the proof by
D
we will denote constants (which can vary from line to line) dependent on the initial data for both problems and by
C_{i}
constants independent on these data. Subtracting (
12
) for the two solutions we obtain

(22)

(x(t)-y(t))^{\prime}=f(x(t))-f(y(t))+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds(x(t)-y(t))+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta^{t}(s)-\xi^{t}(s))\,ds.

We multiply the above equation by
(x(t)-y(t))
, whence

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}\leq|f(x(t))-f(y(t))|\,|x(t)-y(t)|+C_{1}|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+C_{2}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|\,|x(t)-y(t)|.

As from Lemma
3.5
the set
\{\textrm{conv}\{x(t),y(t)\}\,:\ t\geq 0\}
is bounded we deduce that

(23)

\frac{d}{dt}|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}\leq D|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+C_{3}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}.

From Lemma
3.3
we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}\leq C_{4}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|\ \ |(x(t)-y(t))^{\prime}|.

Substituting (
22
) and proceeding similarly as in the proof of (
23
)
it follows that

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}\leq D\ |x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+C_{5}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}.

Combining this inequality with (
23
), we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}(\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}+|x(t)-y(t)|^{2})\leq D|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+C_{6}\|\eta(t)-\xi(t)\|^{2},

which, by the Gronwall lemma yields the required assertion.
∎

3.5.
Existence and uniform boundedness of global attractors.

The question which we address in the remaining part of the article is the following. Assume that
x^{\prime}=f(x)
is a Morse–Smale system. The Morse–Smale property in our case means that the vector field
f
has a finite number of hyperbolic equilibria such that the intersections of stable and unstable manifolds are transversal. If
\varepsilon>0
is small, can we say that the problem with distributed memory has the same structure of the global attractor as the ODE?

The families of maps
\{S^{\varepsilon}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}:L^{2}_{A}(R^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to L^{2}_{A}(R^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the semiflows the govern the solutions of the problem (
12
).

We prove that assumptions (
7
), (
9
), and (
8
) imply that for
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
problems have global attractors
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

(24)

\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0})}\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\ \ \ \textrm{is bounded in}\ L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We denote the set of equilibria by

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{(0,x)\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\ \nabla F(x)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\,x=0\right\}.

Note that for all equilibria
\eta=0
, i.e. the memory variable must be equal to zero.

Lemma 3.7
.

Assume (
7
), (
9
), and (
8
). Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}
such that for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
the problems governed by (
12
)-(
11
) have global attractors
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
, that satisfy (
24
).

Proof.

We first show that if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, then
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is bounded by a bound independent of
\varepsilon
.
We will show that if
(0,x)\in\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
, then
|x|<R
, where
R
is a constant from (
8
). Indeed assume that
|x|\geq R
. Then

C_{F}|x|^{2}\leq-\nabla F(x)\cdot x=\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\,x,x\right)\leq\varepsilon_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds|x|^{2}.

Hence, it suffices to take

\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|ds}{C_{F}}

to arrive at a contradiction.
We continue the argument by using the asymptotic compactness
result for the memory term whose proof is postponed to Appendix 1. We use Lemma
7.1
from that appendix. The asymptotic compactness of the semiflow established in that lemma implies that for every bounded set
\mathcal{B}\subset L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
its
\omega
-limit set
\omega(\mathcal{B})
(see Definition
8.3
in Appendix 1) is nonempty, compact and attracts
\mathcal{B}
in the sense of Hausdorff semidistance in
L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, cf. Lemma
8.4
.

The argument now follows the lines of the proof of Theorem A.3 in
[
3
]
.
Lemma
7.1

as well as the existence of the Lyapunov function imply that for every initial data
(\eta^{0},x_{0})
we can find an equilibrium
(0,x^{*})
such that

S^{\varepsilon}(t)(\eta^{0},x_{0})\to(0,x^{*})
as
t\to\infty
.

Recall, that
L_{\varepsilon}(\eta,x)
defined by (
19
) is Lyapunov function and we define

\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{(\eta,x)\in L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\ L_{\varepsilon}(\eta,x)<\max_{(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}}L_{\varepsilon}(\xi,y)+1\right\}.

As the set
\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
is bounded, so, from the bound (
20
), is the set
\bigcup_{\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]}\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}
.

If we fix
\mathcal{B}
, then there exists time
t^{*}(\mathcal{B})
such that
S^{\varepsilon}(t)\omega(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}
for
t\geq t^{*}
. Indeed, by continuity of
S^{\varepsilon}(t)
for every
p\in\omega(\mathcal{B})
there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{p}
and
t_{p}
such that
S^{\varepsilon}(t_{p})\mathcal{U}_{p}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}
. As
\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}
is positively invariant, the inclusion
S^{\varepsilon}(t)\mathcal{U}_{p}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}
holds for every
t\geq t_{p}
.
Sets
\{\mathcal{U}_{p}\}_{p\in\omega(\mathcal{B})}
are open cover of
\omega(\mathcal{B})
. We extract finite subcover,

\{\mathcal{U}_{p_{n}}\}_{n=1}^{N}
whereas
t^{*}=\max\{t_{p_{1}},\ldots,t_{p_{n}}\}
. Since there exists a function
\psi(t)>0
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\textrm{dist}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(S^{\varepsilon}(t)\mathcal{B},\omega(\mathcal{B}))\leq\lim_{t\to\infty}\psi(t)=0
, for every
t
and
(\eta,x)\in\mathcal{B}
there exists
k(t)\in\omega(\mathcal{B})
and
q(t)
such that
S^{\varepsilon}(t)(\eta,x)=k(t)+q(t)
and
\|q(t)\|_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\leq 2\psi(t)
. Now

S^{\varepsilon}(t+t^{*})(\eta,x)=S^{\varepsilon}(t^{*})k(t)+S^{\varepsilon}(t^{*})(k(t)+q(t))-S^{\varepsilon}(t^{*})k(t)
and
S^{\varepsilon}(t^{*})k(t)\in\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}
. Moreover
S^{\varepsilon}(t^{*})
is continuous and hence it is uniformly continuous in a neighbourhood of a compact set, therefore, for
t
large enough

\|S^{\varepsilon}(t^{*})(k(t)+q(t))-S^{\varepsilon}(t^{*})k(t)\|_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\leq 1.

This means that, for
t
large enough

S_{\varepsilon}(t+t^{*})(\eta,x)
belongs to the ball centered at zero and with radius
\sup_{(\eta,x)\in\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}}\|(\eta,x)\|_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}+1
. Together with Lemma
7.1
it is enough to guarantee the existence of the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
and the bound (
24
).
∎

3.6.
Estimate for the difference of two solutions.

In the next lemma we compare two solutions for different values of
\varepsilon
and initial data. We assume that
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
with
\varepsilon_{0}
being sufficiently small.

Lemma 3.8
.

Consider two solutions: one
(\eta,x)
of problem with
\varepsilon_{1}
and the initial data
(\eta^{0},x_{0})
and another one
(\xi,y)
of the problem with
\varepsilon_{2}
and the initial data
(\xi^{0},y_{0})
.
Then

(25)

|x(t)-y(t)|+\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|\leq Ce^{Ct}(\|\eta^{0}-\xi^{0}\|+|x_{0}-y_{0}|+|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|),

for every
t\geq 0
where the constants
C
depending on the initial data
(\eta^{0},x_{0})
and
(\xi^{0},y_{0})
and are bounded on bounded sets of initial data.

Proof.

Subtracting the equations for
x
and
y
we obtain

\displaystyle(x(t)-y(t))^{\prime}=f(x(t))-f(y(t))+\varepsilon_{1}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)(x(t)-y(t))+(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)y(t)

\displaystyle\ +\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta^{t}(s)-\xi^{t}(s))\,ds+(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\xi^{t}(s)\,ds.

Lyapunov function (
19
) implies that sets
\{\textrm{conv}\{x(t),y(t)\}\,:\ t\geq 0\}
and
\{\textrm{conv}\{\eta^{t},\xi^{t}\}\,:\ t\geq 0\}
are bounded by constants depending on the initial data of the problem. We denote the generic constant depending on the initial data by
C
. Multiplying the above equation by
x(t)-y(t)
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}\leq C\ |x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+C|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|\,|x(t)-y(t)|+C\varepsilon_{1}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|\,|x(t)-y(t)|

\displaystyle\ \ \ \leq C\ |x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+C|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|^{2}+C\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}.

Using (
15
) it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}\leq C\|\xi^{t}-\eta^{t}\|\,|x^{\prime}(t)-y^{\prime}(t)|

\displaystyle\ \ \leq C\|\xi^{t}-\eta^{t}\|\,|x(t)-y(t)|+C|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|\,\|\xi^{t}-\eta^{t}\|+C\varepsilon_{1}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}.

After straightforward calculations, and for sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{0}
,

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}\leq C|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+C|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|^{2},

whence

\frac{d}{dt}(|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2})\leq C\ (|x(t)-y(t)|^{2}+\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}+|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|^{2}),

which yields the assertion of the lemma.
∎

3.7.
Variational equation and
C^{1}
dependence of the solution on initial data.

In the next lemma we characterize the derivative of the flow with respect to the initial data

Lemma 3.9
.

Consider the mapping
L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni(\eta^{0},x_{0})\mapsto(\eta(t),x_{t})=S^{\varepsilon}(t)(\eta^{0},x_{0})
defining the solutions of (
12
)–(
11
). The mapping
S^{\varepsilon}(t)
is Fréchet differentiable and its derivative at
(\eta^{0},x_{0})
is defined as the linear mapping that assigns to
(\xi^{0},w_{0})
the solution of the variational problem

(26)

\displaystyle w^{\prime}(t)=Df(x(t))w(t)+\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)w(t)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\theta^{t}(s)\,ds.

(27)

\displaystyle\theta^{t}(s)=\begin{cases}w(t-s)-w(t)\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ s\leq t\\
w_{0}+\xi^{0}(s-t)-w(t)\ \ \textrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

with the initial data
w(0)=w_{0}
,
\theta^{0}=\xi^{0}
.

Proof.

We take two initial conditions
x_{0},\overline{x}_{0}
and
\eta^{0},\overline{\eta}^{0}
and call the corresponding solutions
(\eta^{t},x(t))
and
(\overline{\eta}^{t},\overline{x}(t))
. Their difference will be called

z(t)=\overline{x}(t)-x(t)
and
\xi^{t}=\overline{\eta}^{t}-{\eta}^{t}
. They satisfy the equations

\displaystyle z^{\prime}(t)=f(x(t)+z(t))-f(x(t))+\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)z(t)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\xi^{t}(s)\,ds.

and

\xi^{t}(s)=\begin{cases}z(t-s)-z(t)\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ s\leq t\\
z_{0}+\xi^{0}(s-t)-z(t)\ \ \textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

This motivates the definition (
26
)–(
27
) of a variational equation with unknowns
\theta^{t}
and
w(t)
.
Denote the difference
z-w=p
and
\xi^{t}-\theta^{t}=\omega^{t}
. Then

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(t)=f(x(t)+z(t))-f(x(t))-Df(x(t))w(t)+\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)p(t)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\omega^{t}(s)\,ds.

\omega^{t}(s)=\begin{cases}p(t-s)-p(t)\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ s\leq t\\
-p(t)\ \ \textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Rearranging the first equation and using the Taylor formula with integral remainder we obtain

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(t)=Df(x(t))p(t)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)D^{2}f(x(t)+\lambda z(t))(z(t),z(t))\,d\lambda+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}M(t-s)p(s)\,ds.

Integrating and using the fact that
x(t)
and
z(t)
are bounded,
D^{2}f
is bounded on bounded sets and
p(0)=0
we obtain

|p(t)|\leq C\int_{0}^{t}|p(s)|ds+C\int_{0}^{t}|z(s)|^{2}\,ds+\varepsilon C\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}|p(r)|\,dr\,ds\leq C\int_{0}^{t}|z(s)|^{2}\,ds+C(1+\varepsilon t)\int_{0}^{t}|p(s)|\,ds.

We need an estimate for
|z(s)|
. We have

\displaystyle z^{\prime}(t)=\int_{0}^{1}Df(x(t)+\lambda z(t))\,d\lambda\,z(t)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}M(s)z(t-s)\,ds+\varepsilon\int_{t}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\,z_{0}+\varepsilon\int_{t}^{\infty}M(s)\xi^{0}(s-t)\,ds.

Rewriting, we obtain

\displaystyle z^{\prime}(t)=\int_{0}^{1}Df(x(t)+\lambda z(t))\,d\lambda\,z(t)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}M(t-s)z(s)\,ds+\varepsilon\int_{t}^{\infty}M(s)\,dsz_{0}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s+t)\xi^{0}(s)\,ds.

Now
x(t)
is bounded and as is
z(t)
because
\overline{x}(t)
is attracted to the attractor and hence also bounded, and
Df
is bounded on bounded sets. We obtain

|z(t)|\leq(1+\varepsilon Ct)|z_{0}|+\varepsilon Ct\|\xi^{0}\|+C(1+\varepsilon t)\int_{0}^{t}|z(s)|\,ds.

By the Gronwall lemma

|z(t)|\leq((1+\varepsilon Ct)|z_{0}|+\varepsilon Ct\|\xi^{0}\|)e^{Ct(1+\varepsilon t)}.

This means that

|z(t)|^{2}\leq g(t)(|z_{0}|^{2}+\|\xi^{0}\|^{2}),

where by
g(t)
we denote a generic increasing and continuous function of
t
.
We deduce that

|p(t)|\leq g(t)(|z_{0}|^{2}+\|\xi^{0}\|^{2})+C(1+\varepsilon t)\int_{0}^{t}|p(s)|\ ds.

By the Gronwall lemma

|p(t)|\leq g(t)(|z_{0}|^{2}+\|\xi^{0}\|^{2}).

Moreover,

\|\omega^{t}\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}(A(s)p(t-s),p(t-s))\,ds-\int_{0}^{\infty}(A(s)p(t),p(t))\,ds,

this means that

\|\omega^{t}\|^{2}\leq g(t)\left(|p(t)|^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}|p(s)|^{2}\,ds\right).

We deduce that

\|\omega^{t}\|\leq g(t)(|z_{0}|^{2}+\|\xi^{0}\|^{2}).

We conclude that

\lim_{|z_{0}|\to 0,\|\xi^{0}\|\to 0}\frac{|p(t)|+\|\omega^{t}\|}{|z_{0}|+\|\xi^{0}\|}\leq\lim_{|z_{0}|\to 0,\|\xi^{0}\|\to 0}\frac{g(t)(|z_{0}|^{2}+\|\xi^{0}\|^{2})}{|z_{0}|+\|\xi^{0}\|}=0.

This implies the Fréchet differentiability of the flow and the fact that the derivative with respect to the initial data is the solution of the variational equation.
∎

The following lemma implies the continuous dependence of the derivative with respect to the initial data on the parameter
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
(\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}},x^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0})
and
(\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}},x^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0})
be the initial data for problems with
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
, respectively. Moreover, let
(\theta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}},w^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0})
and
(\theta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}},w^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0})

be the initial data for the variational problem (
26
)-(
27
). Then

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{DS^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)(\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}},x^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0})}{D(\eta,x)}(\theta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}},w^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0})-\frac{DS^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t)(\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}},x^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0})}{D(\eta,x)}(\theta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}},w^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0})\right\|_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq Ce^{Ct}(|\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}|+|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0}-w^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0}|+\|\theta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}}-\theta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}}\|+|x^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0}-x^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0}|+\|\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}}-\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}}\|),

for every
t\geq 0
, where
C
depends on the initial data in a non-decreasing way and is bounded on bounded sets of initial data.

Proof.

Denote

\frac{DS^{\varepsilon_{i}}(t)(\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{i}},x^{\varepsilon_{i}}_{0})}{D(\eta,x)}(\theta^{0,\varepsilon_{i}},w^{\varepsilon_{i}}_{0})=(\theta^{\varepsilon_{i},t},w^{\varepsilon_{i}}(t)).

We have

\frac{d}{dt}|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|^{2}\leq C|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|^{2}+C\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|^{2}.

Moreover, from (
13
),

\frac{d}{dt}\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|^{2}\leq C\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|\,|(w^{\varepsilon_{2}})^{\prime}(t)|.

It follows that

\frac{d}{dt}\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|^{2}\leq C|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|^{2}+C\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|^{2},

and

\frac{d}{dt}(|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|^{2}+\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|^{2})\leq C(|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|^{2}+\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|^{2}),

whereas

(28)

|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|+\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|\leq Ce^{Ct}(|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0}|+\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},0}\|).

Now, we have the following equation for the difference between two solutions of variational equations along the equations on attractors

\displaystyle(w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)-w^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t))^{\prime}=(Df(x^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t))-Df(x^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t)))w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)+Df(x^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t))(w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)-w^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ +(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1})\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(s)\,dsw^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)+\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds(w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)-w^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ +(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1})\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}(s)\,ds+\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}-\theta^{\varepsilon_{1},t})\,ds.

Denote
w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)-w^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t)=z(t)
and
\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}-\theta^{\varepsilon_{1},t}=\zeta^{t}
. We obtain

\frac{d}{dt}|z(t)|^{2}\leq C|x^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)-x^{\varepsilon_{1}}(t)|\,|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|\,|z(t)|+C|z(t)|^{2}+C|\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}|(|w^{\varepsilon_{2}}(t)|+\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|)|z(t)|+C\varepsilon_{1}\|\zeta^{t}\|\,|z(t)|.

Using (
28
) and Lemma
3.8
we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}|z(t)|^{2}\leq Ce^{Ct}|x^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0}-x^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0}|^{2}+Ce^{Ct}\|\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}}-\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}}\|^{2}+C|z(t)|^{2}+Ce^{Ct}|\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}|^{2}+C\varepsilon_{1}\|\zeta^{t}\|^{2},

where the constants
C
depend on the initial data for original problems and variational problems.
We need to derive the estimate on the difference of the norms
\|\theta^{\varepsilon_{1},t}-\theta^{\varepsilon_{2},t}\|=\|\zeta^{t}\|
. To this end, we use (
15
), whence

\frac{d}{dt}\|\zeta^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\zeta^{t}\|^{2}\leq C\|\zeta^{t}\|\,|z^{\prime}(t)|.

It follows that

\frac{d}{dt}\|\zeta^{t}\|^{2}\leq Ce^{Ct}|x^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0}-x^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0}|^{2}+Ce^{Ct}\|\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}}-\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}}\|^{2}+C|z(t)|^{2}+Ce^{Ct}|\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}|^{2}.

We deduce the estimate

\frac{d}{dt}(|z(t)|^{2}+\|\zeta^{t}\|^{2})\leq C(|z(t)|^{2}+\|\zeta^{t}\|^{2})+Ce^{Ct}(|x^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{0}-x^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{0}|^{2}+\|\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{2}}-\eta^{0,\varepsilon_{1}}\|^{2}+|\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}|^{2}),

and the Gronwall lemma yields the desired assertion.
∎

4.
Continuation of isolating blocks with cone conditions.

The goal of this section is to show that local dynamical properties of (
6
) "survive" as we pass to (
10
).
The dynamical objects whose "survival" we prove are
the isolating blocks satisfying cone conditions that are continued from (
6
). These blocks are certain closed "box-like" neighbourhoods of the equilibria for which the isolation property means that the entry and exit behavior of the dynamical system on the boundary is well defined. The precise definition of isolating blocks as well as the argument that their existence guarantees the existence of local stable and unstable manifolds are moved to Appendix 2. We show here that after extending them by Cartesian product with appropriate ball in the memory variable
\eta
included in the stable variables, the same blocks are valid for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
in (
10
). The continuation of isolating block is established in Section
4.1
. The cone conditions are discussed in Section
4.3
.

4.1.
Isolating block for
\varepsilon=0
.

The next result follows from
[
13
, Theorem 26]
. We give a short proof for the completeness of exposition. In this section we use the notation

B_{u}(\delta)=\prod_{k=1}^{u_{1}}[-\delta,\delta]\times\prod_{k=u_{1}+1}^{u_{1}+u_{2}}\{(x,y)\,:\,x^{2}+y^{2}\leq\delta^{2}\},

and

B_{s}(\delta)=\prod_{k=u_{1}+u_{2}+1}^{u_{1}+u_{2}+s_{1}}[-\delta,\delta]\times\prod_{k=u_{1}+u_{2}+s_{1}+1}^{u_{1}+u_{2}+s_{1}+s_{2}}\{(x,y)\,:\;x^{2}+y^{2}\leq\delta^{2}\}.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
x_{0}
be such that
f(x_{0})=0
. Assume that this equilibrium is hyperbolic, that is, that the spectrum of the matrix
Df(x_{0})
does not intersect the imaginary axis, with
s
equal to the dimension of its stable space, and
u=d-s
the dimension of its unstable space. Let
s=s_{1}+2s_{2}
, where
s_{1}
is the dimension of the generalized eigenspace related with real stable eigenvalues, and
2s_{2}
is the dimension of the generalized eigenspace related with complex stable eigenvalues. Analogously,
u=u_{1}+2u_{2}
. For every sufficiently small constant
\kappa>0
there exists the nonsingular matrix
T_{\kappa}
and a number
\delta_{0}>0
such that for every
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
the set

N_{\kappa}(\delta)=T_{\kappa}\left(B_{u}(\delta)\times B_{s}(\delta)\right)+x_{0}.

is an isolating block with cones for
\varepsilon=0
, i.e. for equation (
6
).

Proof.

We begin with the discussion of the role of the constant
\kappa
. We fix
\kappa>0
, this will be some small number.
Let
T_{\kappa}
be an invertible matrix such that
T_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}
is the Jordan form, that on the diagonal has either real eigenvalues
\lambda
of
Df(x_{0})
, or blocks
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
-\beta&\alpha\end{pmatrix}
in case of complex eigenvalues, and all off-diagonal terms have absolute values not greater than
\kappa
. Assume that the eigenvalues in
T_{\kappa}
are sorted such that: first there are real positive eigenvalues, then complex eigenvalues with positive real part, then negative real eigenvalues, and finally complex eigenvalues with negative real part. Moreover assume that

T_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{pmatrix}
, where
A+A^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{u\times u}
is negative definite and
B+B^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times s}
is positive definite. For every
\kappa>0
such change of coordinates
T_{\kappa}
exists. We first prove that the set
N_{\kappa}(\delta)

is an isolating block for sufficiently small
\kappa
.

If we denote
x=x_{0}+T_{\kappa}y
, we obtain the system

y^{\prime}=T_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}y+T_{\kappa}^{-1}f(x_{0}+T_{\kappa}y)-T_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}y=h(y).

Now for

y\in B_{u}(2\delta)\times B_{s}(2\delta),
we deduce, by the Taylor theorem, as
f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
that

|T_{\kappa}^{-1}f(x_{0}+T_{\kappa}y)-T_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}y|\leq C_{\kappa}\delta^{2}

where
C_{\kappa}
depends on
\kappa
but not on
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
.
We need to prove that:

•

if

y\in B_{u}(2\delta)\times\partial B_{s}(\delta),

then

(29)

h_{i}(y)y_{i}<0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{u_{1}+2u_{2}+1,\ldots,u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}\}

and

(30)

h_{i}(y)y_{i}+h_{i+1}(y)y_{i+1}<0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}+1,\ldots,u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}+j,\ldots u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}+(2s_{2}-1)\},

where
j
are odd numbers,

•

if

y\in\left(B_{u}(2\delta)\setminus(\textrm{int}B_{u}(\delta))\right)\times B_{s}(\delta),

then

(31)

h_{i}(y)y_{i}>0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{1,\ldots,u_{1}\}

and

(32)

h_{i}(y)y_{i}+h_{i+1}(y)y_{i+1}>0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{u_{1}+1,\ldots,u_{1}+j,\ldots u_{1}+2u_{2}-1\},

where
j
are odd numbers,

By the Lipschitz continuous dependence on the initial condition, on bounded sets of initial data and compact time intervals these conditions imply the isolation given in Definition
8.6
.

To prove the first assertion observe that for
i\in u_{1}+u_{2}+1,\ldots,u_{1}+u_{2}+s_{1}
.

h_{i}(y)y_{i}=\lambda_{i}y_{i}^{2}+G(y),

where

|G(y)|\leq(d-1)\kappa 4|\delta|^{2}+2C_{\kappa}|\delta|^{3},

the first term coming from off diagonal values (at most
d-1
) in
T_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}
, and the second one from the remainder which is a product of number which is dominated by the euclidean norm of a vector bounded by
C_{\kappa}|\delta|^{2}
and a number bounded by
2\delta
. This means that we can choose
\kappa
small enough (related to the lowest eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
) and
\delta_{0}
(that is chosen according to
C_{\kappa}
) and get (
29
).

For the complex pairs of eigenvalues, the off diagonal terms in blocks
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
-\beta&\alpha\end{pmatrix}
cancel and we obtain

h_{i}(y)y_{i}+h_{i+1}(y)y_{i+1}=\textrm{Re}\,\lambda_{i}(y_{i}^{2}+y_{i+1}^{2})+G(y),

with

|G(y)|\leq 8(d-2)\kappa|\delta|^{2}+2C_{k}|\delta|^{3},

and (
30
) holds analogously as (
29
). Verification of (
31
) and (
32
) follows analogously.

To see that the cone condition holds it is enough to take the matrix
Q
such that
q_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
,
q_{ii}=-1
for
i=1,\ldots,u
and
q_{ii}=1
for
i=u+1,\ldots,d
and see that
QT_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}+T_{\kappa}^{-1}Df(x_{0})T_{\kappa}Q
is positive definite, which must be preserved on a small neighborhood of
y=0
.
∎

4.2.
Continuation of isolation property for
\varepsilon>0

In the subsequent part of this section we will show that it is possible to choose
\delta
and
\kappa
as well as
R>0
such that if
N_{\kappa}(\delta)
is an isolating block with cones for (
6
) then the set
\overline{B}_{L_{A}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times N_{\kappa}(\delta)
is an isolating block with cones for (
10
). We start from an estimate. Substitute (
12
) in (
13
).
Then we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}\leq-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,f(x(t))\right)-2\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)x(t)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -2\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds,\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds\right).

After computations which use Lemmas
2.4
and
2.7
it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}+C_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}\leq 2D_{A}\|\eta^{t}\||f(x(t))|+2\varepsilon D_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds|x(t)|+2\varepsilon D_{A}D_{M}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}\leq\|\eta^{t}\|\left(2D_{A}|f(x(t))|+2\varepsilon D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds|x(t)|+2\varepsilon D_{A}D_{M}\|\eta^{t}\|-C_{A}\|\eta^{t}\|\right).

The above computation leads is a straightforward way to the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that

f(x_{0})=0
and that
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a compact set containing
x_{0}
. Moreover let

\varepsilon<\frac{C_{A}}{2DAD_{M}}
and

R>\frac{2D_{A}\left(\sup_{z\in N}|f(z)|+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\cdot\sup_{z\in N}|z|\right)}{C_{A}-2\varepsilon D_{A}D_{M}}.

Then for
\eta\in\partial B_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}}(0,R)
, and
y\in N
there holds

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}}<0.

In the next theorem we demonstrate that the entry and exit behavior established in Lemma
4.1
are preserved for
\varepsilon>0
. The new isolating block will have the form

(0,x_{0})+\overline{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times T_{\kappa}(B_{u}(\delta)\times B_{s}(\delta)).

The entry set will be given by

(0,x_{0})+\partial{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times T_{\kappa}(B_{u}(\delta)\times\partial B_{s}(\delta))

and the exit set by

(0,x_{0})+\overline{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times T_{\kappa}(\partial B_{u}(\delta)\times B_{s}(\delta)).

To this end let us first rewrite the equation (
10
) in the changed variables
y
.

y^{\prime}(t)=h(y(t))+\varepsilon T_{\kappa}^{-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)(x_{0}+T_{\kappa}y(t))+\varepsilon T_{\kappa}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds.

Now choose
\kappa
and
\delta_{0}
such that Lemma
4.1
holds and assume that
\delta<\frac{\delta_{0}}{2}
. For such
\delta
let
r(\delta)
be a smallest possible number such that
N_{\kappa}(2\delta)\subset B(x_{0},r)
. Note that
r\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
. Take
\eta^{t}\in\overline{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{n}}(0,R)
and
x=T_{\kappa}y+x_{0}\in N_{\kappa}(2\delta)
. We rewrite the
i
-th equation of the above system as

y_{i}^{\prime}(t)=h_{i}(y(t))+g_{i}(\eta^{t},y(t)),

where

g_{i}(\eta^{t},y(t))=\varepsilon\left(T_{\kappa}^{-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)(x_{0}+T_{\kappa}y(t))\right)_{i}+\varepsilon\left(T_{\kappa}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta^{t}(s)\,ds\right)_{i}

hence

(33)

|g_{i}(\eta,y)|\leq|g(\eta,y)|\leq\varepsilon\|T_{\kappa}^{-1}\|D_{M}\|\eta^{t}\|+\varepsilon\|T_{\kappa}^{-1}\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds(|x_{0}|+r)\leq\varepsilon C_{1}(R+|x_{0}|+r),

for a constant
C_{1}>0
. Now we are ready to state our result about the continuation of the isolating block for
\varepsilon>0
.

Theorem 4.3
.

There exists
\kappa>0
,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
\delta>0
, and
R>0
such that for every fixed point
x_{0}
, every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
the set
\overline{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times N_{\kappa}(\delta)
is an isolating block for (
10
), i.e.

(I)

we have the entry behavior on
\partial B_{s}(\delta)
, that is, if

(\eta,y)\in\overline{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times B_{u}(2\delta)\times\partial B_{s}(\delta),

then

(34)

(h_{i}(y)+g_{i}(\eta,y))y_{i}<0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{u_{1}+2u_{2}+1,\ldots,u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}\},

and

(35)

\displaystyle(h_{i}(y)+g_{i}(\eta,y))y_{i}+(h_{i+1}(y)+g_{i+1}(\eta,y))y_{i+1}<0

\displaystyle\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}+1,\ldots,u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}+j,\ldots,u_{1}+2u_{2}+s_{1}+(2s_{2}-1)\},

where
j
are odd numbers,

(II)

we have the exit behavior on
\partial B_{u}(\delta)
, that is, if

(\eta,y)\in\overline{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times\left(B_{u}(2\delta)\setminus(\textrm{int}B_{u}(\delta))\right)\times B_{s}(\delta),

then

(36)

(h_{i}(y)+g_{i}(\eta,y))y_{i}>0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{1,\ldots,u_{1}\},

and

(37)

(h_{i}(y)+g_{i}(\eta,y))y_{i}+(h_{i+1}(y)+g_{i+1}(\eta,y))y_{i+1}>0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ i\in\{u_{1}+1,\ldots,u_{1}+j,\ldots u_{1}+2u_{2}-1\},

where
j
are odd numbers,

(III)

we have the entry behavior on
\partial B_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{d}}(0,R)
, that is, if
(\eta,y)\in\partial B_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{d}}(0,R)\times B_{u}(2\delta)\times B_{s}(\delta)
, then

\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{d}}<0\ \ \textrm{at}\ \ t=0.\,

Proof.

We first provide the condition needed for (III) to hold: this is the entry condition for the variable
\eta
. Following Lemma
4.2
we need that
R>0
and
\delta_{0}>0
should satisfy

(38)

R>\frac{2D_{A}\left(\sup_{z\in N_{\kappa}(2\delta)}\|Df(z)\|r(\delta)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,dsr(\delta)\right)}{C_{A}-2\varepsilon D_{A}D_{M}}.

We switch to the conditions needed for (I) and (II), that is, for (
34
)–(
37
).

We first note that there exists
\delta_{0}
and a constant
C_{2}>0
such that if only
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
and
y\in B_{u}(2\delta)\times\partial B_{s}(\delta)\cup(B_{u}(2\delta)\setminus\textrm{int}B_{u}(\delta))\times B_{s}(\delta)
then

(39)

C_{2}\delta\leq|h_{i}(y)|\ \ \textrm{for indexes corresponding to real eigenvalues},

and

(40)

C_{2}\delta^{2}\leq|h_{i}(y)y_{i}+h_{i+1}(y)y_{i+1}|\ \ \textrm{for indexes corresponding to complex eigenvalues}.

In order to guarantee (
34
) and (
36
) we need
h_{i}(y)
to have the same sign as and
h_{i}(y)+g_{i}(\eta,y)
for
y\in B_{u}(2\delta)\times\partial B_{s}(\delta)\cup(B_{u}(2\delta)\setminus\textrm{int}B_{u}(\delta))\times B_{s}(\delta)
and
\eta\in\overline{B}_{L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{d}}(0,R)
.
For the complex eigenvalues we need, on the other hand,
that
(h_{i}(y)+g_{i}(\eta,y))y_{i}+(h_{i+1}(y)+g_{i+1}(\eta,y))y_{i+1}
and
h_{i}(y)y_{i}+h_{i+1}(y)y_{i+1}
have the same signs. Therefore, in view of
(
39
) it is sufficient to prove that
|g_{i}(\eta,y)|<C_{2}\delta
and
|g_{i}(\eta,y)y_{i}+g_{i+1}(\eta,y)y_{i+1}|<C_{2}\delta^{2}
. Using (
33
) it is enough if the following inequality holds

(41)

\varepsilon C_{1}(R+|x_{0}|+r(\delta))<C_{2}\delta.

First choose
\varepsilon_{0}
such that
C_{A}-2\varepsilon_{0}D_{A}D_{M}>\frac{C_{A}}{2}
. We need to guarantee (
38
). Note that if it holds for
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}
then the same inequality holds for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
.
Now pick
R>0
. We need to choose
\delta
small enough such that

\frac{4D_{A}\sup_{z\in N_{\kappa}(2\delta)}\|Df(z)\|r(\delta)}{C_{A}}<\frac{R}{2}.

This is possible, because by decreasing
\delta
we can make
r(\delta)
arbitrarily small. Now it is possible to choose sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{0}
such that
\varepsilon_{0}C_{1}(R+|x_{0}|+r(\delta))<C_{2}\delta
and
\frac{\varepsilon_{0}4D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\,r(\delta)}{C_{A}}<\frac{R}{2}
. Thus both inequalities are satisfied and the proof is complete.
∎

4.3.
Continuation of cone condition for
\varepsilon>0
.

The goal of this section is to show that cone conditions from (
6
) "survive" for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
for (
10
) on the same isolating block. We refer to Appendix 2 for the definition of the cone condition. We assume that it holds for the equation without memory, that is

x^{\prime}(t)=f^{\varepsilon}(x(t))=f(x(t))+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,dsx(t).

For this equation we have a quadratic form
Q
(a symmetric matrix) and a set
N
(isolating block) such that
on
N
we have for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
|\varepsilon|\leq\Delta
for some
G>0

(42)

x^{t}(Df^{\varepsilon}(N)^{T}Q+QDf^{\varepsilon}(N))x\geq G|x|^{2}.

Note that

Df^{\varepsilon}(x)=Df(x)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds.

Hence, if
\varepsilon>0
is small enough, then the same form
Q
is valid both for
f
and for
f^{\varepsilon}
(with possibly smaller constant
G
).
Let
(\eta,x)
and
(\xi,y)
be two solutions of (
12
)–(
11
) such
x,y\in N
and
\|\eta\|,\|\xi\|\leq R
.
Let
E>0
be any positive constant. We hope that for the quadratic form

(43)

\widetilde{Q}(\eta,x)=Q(x)-E\|\eta\|^{2}

we will have cone-conditions on the set (isolated block)

(44)

\widetilde{N}=\{\|\eta\|\leq R\}\times N.

We have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(Q(x(t)-y(t),x(t)-y(t))\right)=(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})^{\top}Q(x-y)+(x-y)^{\top}Q(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}).

Since

\displaystyle x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(f^{\varepsilon}(x)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta(s)ds\right)-\left(f^{\varepsilon}(y)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\xi(s)ds\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(f^{\varepsilon}(x)-f^{\varepsilon}(y))+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{Df^{\varepsilon}[x,y]}(x-y)+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds,

we obtain using Lemma
2.7

\displaystyle(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})^{\top}Q(x-y)+(x-y)^{\top}Q(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})=(x-y)^{\top}\left(\overline{Df^{\varepsilon}[x,y]}^{\top}Q+Q\overline{Df^{\varepsilon}[x,y]}\right)(x-y)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds\right)^{\top}Q(x-y)+\varepsilon(x-y)^{T}Q\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds

\displaystyle\ \geq G|x-y|^{2}-2\varepsilon D_{M}\|Q\|\cdot|x-y|\cdot\|\eta-\xi\|.

From Lemma
3.3
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}\leq-C_{A}\|\eta-\xi\|^{2}-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds,(x(t)-y(t))^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \leq-C_{A}\|\eta-\xi\|^{2}-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds,\overline{Df^{\varepsilon}[x,y]}(x-y)\right)+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ -2\varepsilon\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds,\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta(s)-\xi(s))ds\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \leq-C_{A}\|\eta-\xi\|^{2}+2D_{A}\|\eta-\xi\|\cdot\|Df^{\varepsilon}(N)\|\cdot|x-y|+\varepsilon 2D_{A}D_{M}\|\eta-\xi\|^{2}

Now we are ready to demonstrate that the cone condition holds. From previous derivations we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(Q(x(t)-y(t),x(t)-y(t))-E\|\eta^{t}-\xi^{t}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \geq G|x-y|^{2}-2\varepsilon D_{M}\|Q\|\cdot|x-y|\cdot\|\eta-\xi\|

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ -E\left(-C_{A}\|\eta-\xi\|^{2}+2D_{A}\|\eta-\xi\|\cdot\|Df^{\varepsilon}(N)\|\cdot|x-y|+\varepsilon 2D_{M}D_{A}\|\eta-\xi\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ =G|x-y|^{2}+2\left(-\varepsilon D_{M}\|Q\|-ED_{A}\|Df^{\varepsilon}(N)\|\right)|x-y|\cdot\|\eta-\xi\|

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ +\left(C_{A}E-2\varepsilon D_{A}D_{M}\right)\|\eta-\xi\|^{2}

The expression on the right-hand side is a quadratic form in terms of
(|x-y|,\|\eta-\xi\|)
with the matrix

(45)

B=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}G&\left(-\varepsilon D_{M}\|Q\|-ED_{A}\|Df^{\varepsilon}(N)\|\right)\\
\left(-\varepsilon D_{M}\|Q\|-ED_{A}\|Df^{\varepsilon}(N)\|\right)&\left(C_{A}E-2\varepsilon D_{A}D_{M}\right)\\
\end{array}\right]

Consider first the case with
\varepsilon=0
. Matrix
B
becomes

(46)

B_{0}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}G&-ED_{A}\|Df(N)\|\\
-ED_{A}\|Df(N)\|&C_{A}E\\
\end{array}\right]

It is positive definite provided the following condition holds

(47)

0<\det(B_{0})=C_{A}EG-E^{2}D_{A}^{2}\|Df(N)\|^{2},

which is satisfied if

(48)

E<\frac{C_{A}G}{D_{A}^{2}\|Df(N)\|^{2}}.

Since
\det B
depends continuously on
\varepsilon
we obtain the following theorem.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
E>0
satisfying

(49)

E<\frac{C_{A}G}{D_{A}^{2}\|Df(N)\|^{2}}.

there exists
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(E)
, such that cone condition holds for quadratic form (
43
) for any
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
.

The next result will be needed for the continuous dependence of local stable and unstable manifolds on
\varepsilon
. We obtain this continuity using the abstract results from Appendix 3. To use them, we must verify the cone conditions with parameter given in Definition
9.1
. Consider the problem governed by (
12
)–(
11
) and
denote the solution with
\varepsilon\in[0,\Delta]
and initial data
(\eta^{0},x_{0})\in\widetilde{N}
by
S^{\varepsilon}(t)(\eta^{0},x_{0})=(\eta^{t}_{\varepsilon,x_{0},\eta_{0}},x_{\varepsilon,x_{0},\eta_{0}})
.
Define

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}(x,\eta,\varepsilon)=\widetilde{Q}(x,\eta)+L|\varepsilon|^{2}={Q}(x)-E\|\eta\|^{2}+L|\varepsilon|^{2},

where
L
can be either positive or negative constant. In the next lemma we prove that it is possible to choose this constant in such a way, that
\widehat{Q}
satisfies the conditions given in Definition
9.1
. If
L
is a large positive number then we obtain continuous dependence of unstable manifold on
\varepsilon
and if
L
is a large negative number then we obtain the continuous dependence of stable manifold on
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 4.5
.

There exists
L_{0}>0
and
E_{max}>0
such that for every
|L|\geq L_{0}
and
E\in(0,E_{max})
there exists
\Delta(E)>0
such that the cone conditions with parameter given in (i) and (ii) of Definition
9.1
are satisfied on the h-set
\widetilde{N}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in[0,\Delta(E)]

Proof.

Assume that
(\eta^{0}_{1},x^{0}_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
and
(\eta^{0}_{2},x^{0}_{2},\varepsilon_{2})
are such that

\widehat{Q}(x^{0}_{1}-x^{0}_{2},\eta^{0}_{1}-\eta^{0}_{2},\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})=0.

We must prove that

\frac{d}{dt}\widehat{Q}(x_{\varepsilon_{1},x^{0}_{1}\eta^{0}_{1}}(t)-x_{\varepsilon_{2},x^{0}_{2}\eta^{0}_{2}}(t),\eta_{\varepsilon_{1},x_{1}^{0},\eta_{1}^{0}}(t)-\eta_{\varepsilon_{2},x^{0}_{2}\eta^{0}_{2}}(t),\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})\geq 0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ t=0.

Denoting, for simplicity,
(\eta_{1}(t),x_{1}(t))=(\eta_{\varepsilon_{1},x_{1}^{0},\eta_{1}^{0}}(t),x_{\varepsilon_{1},x^{0}_{1}\eta^{0}_{1}}(t))
and

(\eta_{2}(t),x_{2}(t))=(\eta_{\varepsilon_{2},x_{2}^{0},\eta_{2}^{0}}(t),x_{\varepsilon_{2},x^{0}_{2}\eta^{0}_{2}}(t))
we should prove that

(50)

\frac{d}{dt}\left((x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))-E\|\eta_{1}(t)-\eta_{2}(t)\|^{2}\right)\geq 0\ \ \textrm{for}\ \ t=0.

We estimate both terms from below separately

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}((x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))=(x_{1}^{\prime}(t)-x_{2}^{\prime}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))+(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}^{\prime}(t)-x_{2}^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ \ =(f(x_{1}(t))-f(x_{2}(t)))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))+(\varepsilon_{1}x_{1}(t)-\varepsilon_{2}x_{2}(t))^{\top}\int_{0}^{\infty}M^{\top}(s)\,dsQ(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ \ \qquad+\left(\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{1}^{t}(s)\,ds-\varepsilon_{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{2}^{t}(s)\ ds\right)^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ \ \qquad+(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(f(x_{1}(t))-f(x_{2}(t)))+(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds(\varepsilon_{1}x_{1}(t)-\varepsilon_{2}x_{2}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ \ \qquad+(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q\left(\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{1}^{t}(s)\,ds-\varepsilon_{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{2}^{t}(s)\ ds\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ =(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}(Df(N)^{\top}Q+QDf(N))(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ \ \qquad+\varepsilon_{2}(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M^{\top}(s)\,dsQ+Q\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds\right)(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))

\displaystyle\ \ \ \qquad+2\varepsilon_{2}(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))\,ds\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \qquad+2(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,dsx_{1}(t)+2(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{1}^{t}(s)\,ds\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ =I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}.

Now

I_{1}+I_{2}\geq G|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|^{2},

where
G
can be chosen uniformly for
\varepsilon\in[0,\Delta].

Moreover

I_{3}\geq-2\Delta|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|\ \|Q\|D_{M}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|,

and

I_{4}\geq-2|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|\ |x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|\ \|Q\|\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\sup_{x\in N}|x|+D_{M}R\right).

For simplicity we use the following notation for the constant which will appear several times in the subsequent computations
\overline{R}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\sup_{x\in N}|x|+D_{M}R
.
Summarizing, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}((x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))\geq G|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|^{2}-2\Delta|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|\,\|Q\|D_{M}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\ \ \ -2|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|\,|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|\,\|Q\|\overline{R}.

We estimate the second term in (
50
) from Lemma
3.3

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|^{2}\leq-C_{A}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|^{2}-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))ds,(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ =-C_{A}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|^{2}-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))ds,f(x_{1}(t))-f(x_{2}(t))\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))ds,\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,dsx_{1}(t)-\varepsilon_{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,dsx_{2}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ -2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))ds,\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{1}^{t}(s)\,ds-\varepsilon_{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{2}^{t}(s)ds\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ =-C_{A}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|^{2}-2\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))ds,f(x_{1}(t))-f(x_{2}(t))\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -2(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))ds,\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,dsx_{1}(t)+\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\eta_{1}^{t}(s)\,ds\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -2\varepsilon_{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}A(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))ds,\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)\,ds(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))+\int_{0}^{\infty}M(s)(\eta_{1}^{t}(s)-\eta_{2}^{t}(s))\,ds\right).

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|^{2}\leq-C_{A}\|\eta^{t}_{1}-\eta^{t}_{2}\|^{2}+2D_{A}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|\cdot\|Df(N)\|\cdot|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|

\displaystyle\ \ \ +2|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|D_{A}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|\overline{R}+2\Delta D_{M}D_{A}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|^{2}+2\Delta D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|\ |x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|.

Putting together the two estimates we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left((x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))-E\|\eta_{1}(t)-\eta_{2}(t)\|^{2}\right)\geq

\displaystyle\ \ \ \geq G|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|^{2}+E(C_{A}-2\Delta D_{M}D_{A})\|\eta^{t}_{1}-\eta^{t}_{2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ -2|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|\left(\Delta\|Q\|D_{M}+ED_{A}\|Df(N)\|+E\Delta D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\right)\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\ \ \ -2|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|\,|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|\,\|Q\|\overline{R}-2E|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|D_{A}\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|\overline{R}.

We need the right-hand side of the last estimate to be bounded from below by
0
at
t=0
, on the boundary of the cone, i.e. for
{Q}(x^{0}_{1}-x^{0}_{2})-E\|\eta^{0}_{1}-\eta_{2}^{0}\|^{2}+L|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|^{2}=0
, whereas we can estimate from above as follows

|\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|L|}}\left(\sqrt{\|Q\|}\cdot|x^{0}_{1}-x^{0}_{2}|+\sqrt{E}\|\eta^{0}_{1}-\eta_{2}^{0}\|\right)

We deduce that, at
t=0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left((x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))-E\|\eta_{1}(t)-\eta_{2}(t)\|^{2}\right)|_{t=0}\geq\left(G-\frac{2\|Q\|^{\frac{3}{2}}\overline{R}}{\sqrt{|L|}}\right)|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +E\left(C_{A}-2\Delta D_{A}D_{M}-\frac{2\sqrt{E}D_{A}\overline{R}}{\sqrt{|L|}}\right)\|\eta^{t}_{1}-\eta^{t}_{2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ -2|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}|\left(\Delta\|Q\|D_{M}+ED_{A}\|Df(N)\|+E\Delta D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds+\frac{E\sqrt{\|Q\|}D_{A}\overline{R}}{\sqrt{|L|}}+\frac{\|Q\|\overline{R}\sqrt{E}}{\sqrt{|L|}}\right)\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|.

We are free to choose sufficiently large (positive or negative)
L
, sufficiently small
\Delta
and sufficiently small
E
. We already have the upper bound on
E
in Lemma
4.4
given by
E\leq E_{max}
.

Now suppose that
|L|
is large enough and
\Delta
is small enough such that

\sqrt{|L|}\geq\max\left\{\frac{4\|Q\|^{\frac{3}{2}}\overline{R}}{G},\frac{8\sqrt{E_{max}}D_{A}\overline{R}}{C_{A}}\right\}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\Delta\leq\frac{C_{A}}{8D_{A}D_{M}}.

With these assumption the above estimate takes the form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left((x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))^{\top}Q(x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t))-E\|\eta_{1}(t)-\eta_{2}(t)\|^{2}\right)|_{t=0}\geq\frac{G}{2}|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}|^{2}+\frac{EC_{A}}{2}\|\eta^{t}_{1}-\eta^{t}_{2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -2|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}|\left(\Delta\|Q\|D_{M}+ED_{A}\|Df(N)\|+E\Delta D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds+\frac{E\sqrt{\|Q\|}D_{A}\overline{R}}{\sqrt{|L|}}+\frac{\|Q\|\overline{R}\sqrt{E}}{\sqrt{|L|}}\right)\|\eta_{1}^{t}-\eta_{2}^{t}\|.

The above quadratic form on
|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}|
and
\|\eta^{t}_{1}-\eta^{t}_{2}\|
is nonnegatively defined provided

\frac{EC_{A}G}{4}\geq\left(\Delta\|Q\|D_{M}+ED_{A}\|Df(N)\|+E\Delta D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds+\frac{E\sqrt{\|Q\|}D_{A}\overline{R}}{\sqrt{|L|}}+\frac{\|Q\|\overline{R}\sqrt{E}}{\sqrt{|L|}}\right)^{2}.

But we know that

\displaystyle\left(\Delta\|Q\|D_{M}+ED_{A}\|Df(N)\|+E\Delta D_{A}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds+\frac{E\sqrt{\|Q\|}D_{A}\overline{R}}{\sqrt{|L|}}+\frac{\|Q\|\overline{R}\sqrt{E}}{\sqrt{|L|}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq 5\Delta^{2}\|Q\|^{2}D_{M}^{2}+5E^{2}D_{A}^{2}\|Df(N)\|^{2}+5E^{2}\Delta^{2}D_{A}^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\right)^{2}+\frac{5E^{2}\|Q\|D_{A}^{2}\overline{R}^{2}}{|L|}+\frac{5\|Q\|^{2}\overline{R}^{2}{E}}{|L|}.

Hence we need the following five inequalities

\displaystyle 5\Delta^{2}\|Q\|^{2}D_{M}^{2}\leq\frac{EC_{A}G}{20},\ \ 5E^{2}D_{A}^{2}\|Df(N)\|^{2}\leq\frac{EC_{A}G}{20},\ \ 5E^{2}\Delta^{2}D_{A}^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\right)^{2}\leq\frac{EC_{A}G}{20},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \frac{5E^{2}\|Q\|D_{A}^{2}\overline{R}^{2}}{|L|}\leq\frac{EC_{A}G}{20},\ \ \frac{5\|Q\|^{2}\overline{R}^{2}{E}}{|L|}\leq\frac{EC_{A}G}{20},

or, after the simplification,

\displaystyle 100\Delta^{2}\|Q\|^{2}D_{M}^{2}\leq EC_{A}G,\ \ 100ED_{A}^{2}\|Df(N)\|^{2}\leq C_{A}G,\ \ 100E\Delta^{2}D_{A}^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\right)^{2}\leq C_{A}G,

\displaystyle\ \ \ {100E\|Q\|D_{A}^{2}\overline{R}^{2}}\leq{C_{A}G|L|},\ \ {100\|Q\|^{2}\overline{R}^{2}}\leq{C_{A}G}{|L|}.

We see that it is enough to choose

|L|\geq\max\left\{\frac{100E_{max}\|Q\|D_{A}^{2}\overline{R}^{2}}{C_{A}G},\frac{100\|Q\|^{2}\overline{R}^{2}}{C_{A}G}\right\}

and the last two inequalities hold. We are now free to pick
E
which satisfies

0<E\leq\frac{C_{A}G}{100D_{A}^{2}\|Df(N)\|^{2}}\ \ \textrm{and}\ \ E<E_{max},

and we finally need to pick
\Delta
such that

\Delta^{2}\leq\min\left\{\frac{EC_{A}G}{100\|Q\|^{2}D_{M}^{2}},\frac{C_{A}G}{100ED_{A}^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|M(s)\|\,ds\right)^{2}}\right\}.

The proof is complete.
∎

5.
Equilibria, persistence of connections in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.

In this section we relate the equilibria of the ODE (
6
) with the equilibria of the problem (
12
)–(
11
). We show that if
\varepsilon
is small, then for every equilibrium
e
of (
6
) there exists an equilibrium
(0,e^{\varepsilon})
of (
12
)–(
11
) in its vicinity and the perturbed system has no other equilibria. Moreover, we show the upper semi-continuity result on the connections between the equilibria, that is, if the two equilibria of (
12
)–(
11
) are connected for a sequence of parameters
\varepsilon\to 0
, then the connection also exists for
\varepsilon=0
. We remind that the limit equation (
6
)
has only a finite number of isolated and hyperbolic equilibria and the system is Morse–Smale, i.e. the intersections of their stable and unstable manifolds are always transversal.

5.1.
Continuation of equilibria

We begin from the result on the continuation of the equilibria of the system from
\varepsilon=0
to the positive values.

Theorem 5.1
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
R>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0})
if
e\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an equilibrium for (
6
) with an isolating block with cones
N^{x}
then the problem governed by (
12
)–(
11
) has an equilibrium
(0,e^{\varepsilon})
which is unique in the set
\widetilde{N}^{x}=\{\|\eta\|\leq R\}\times N^{x}
. Moreover
(0,e^{\varepsilon})
are the only equilibria for (
12
)–(
11
).

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}
the set of equilibria of (
6
) and let
e\in\mathcal{E}
. Take
R
and
N^{x}
from Theorem
4.3
(isolating set) and take
E
satisfying the constraints from Lemma
4.4
(the cone condition), Lemma
4.5
(the cone condition with parameters) and Lemma
